View View Press play to listen to this content SEXUAL dysfunction in chronic hepatitis B is common, multifactorial, and frequently missed in routine clinical care. A narrative review found prevalence estimates ranging from 8.6% to 63.1% across heterogeneous studies of hepatitis B virus related disease. In one larger chronic hepatitis B cohort, 25.4% of patients experienced sexual dysfunction, with higher rates among those with poorer liver function, greater fibrosis, and more severe depression. Sexual Dysfunction in Chronic Hepatitis B Has Multiple Drivers. The review presents sexual dysfunction in chronic hepatitis B as the product of interacting biological, psychological, and social factors. Liver dysfunction may disturb sex hormone metabolism, while chronic inflammation, endothelial impairment, neuropathy, and cardiometabolic comorbidities may further affect sexual response. In men, erectile dysfunction is the most frequently studied manifestation. Women may experience reduced desire, impaired arousal or lubrication, dyspareunia, and orgasmic difficulties, although female specific evidence remains limited. Anxiety, depression, concerns about viral transmission, stigma, and relationship strain may also reduce desire and intimacy. Among patients receiving oral antiviral therapy, reported prevalence reached 33.0% for depression and 38.3% for anxiety. Antiviral Effects Require Careful Interpretation. Available evidence does not show a consistent direct association between nucleos(t)ide analogues and sexual dysfunction. When symptoms develop during therapy, clinicians should assess liver disease progression, hypogonadism, psychological distress, diabetes, and other comorbidities before attributing them to treatment. Interferon based regimens may contribute indirectly through fatigue and neuropsychiatric adverse effects. However, chronic hepatitis B specific evidence remains limited. Screening Could Close a Clinical Care Gap. The proposed pathway recommends annual or twice yearly screening using validated instruments, including the International Index of Erectile Function and Female Sexual Function Index. Positive results should prompt a nonjudgmental sexual history and assessment of endocrine, psychological, relational, and lifestyle factors. Management should be individualized and may include optimizing liver disease control, addressing cardiometabolic conditions, treating specific sexual symptoms, providing psychological support, and encouraging appropriate lifestyle changes. The authors emphasized that several interventions are extrapolated from other chronic disease populations because randomized trials in chronic hepatitis B are lacking. Future studies should use standardized, sex specific measures and include sexual health outcomes in antiviral trials. Reference Muzammal S et al. Location and population
Global population of adults with chronic hepatitis B infection; specific cohorts include patients with poorer liver function, greater fibrosis, and severe depression.

A narrative review reporting that sexual dysfunction is common, multifactorial, and frequently missed in routine care for chronic hepatitis B, with prevalence estimates ranging from 8.6% to 63.1%.

Among patients on oral antiviral therapy, depression prevalence reached 33.0% and anxiety 38.3%.
Response, status and uncertainty
Authors recommend annual or twice-yearly screening using validated instruments (e.g., International Index of Erectile Function, Female Sexual Function Index) and individualized management addressing liver disease control, cardiometabolic conditions, psychological support, and lifestyle changes. Evidence specific to chronic hepatitis B remains limited, and several interventions are extrapolated from other chronic disease populations. Randomized trials in chronic hepatitis B are lacking.

利益相关者敦促进行更广泛的筛查,疫苗接种 以遏制尼日利亚的乙型肝炎负担 - 尼日利亚卫报新闻

Stakeholders urge wider screening, vaccination to curb Nigeria’s Hepatitis B burden - The Guardian Nigeria News
新闻简报On 15 August 2026, Nigerian health stakeholders urged wider hepatitis B screening, vaccination and public education to curb an estimated 18.2 million chronic cases nationwide. Speaking during a World Hepatitis Day event in Mushin, Lagos State, Cerba Lancet’s CEO cited an 8–9% prevalence and about 325,000 new HBV infections in 2022. The community screening in Mushin Local Government attracted 247 participants and offered on-site testing, referrals and support. Officials emphasized early detection to prevent liver cancer and highlighted transmission routes including blood contact, unprotected sex and mother-to-child spread. While a vaccine exists, organizers stressed the need for stronger advocacy and culturally adapted education to close immunity gaps.

An estimated 18.2 million Nigerians have chronic hepatitis B; prevalence about 8–9% of the population; approximately 325,000 new HBV infections estimated in Nigeria in 2022; 247 residents screened during the event.

Stakeholders urge wider screening, vaccination to curb Nigeria’s Hepatitis B burden - The Guardian Nigeria News

News Brief
On 15 August 2026, Nigerian health stakeholders urged wider hepatitis B screening, vaccination and public education to curb an estimated 18.2 million chronic cases nationwide. Speaking during a World Hepatitis Day event in Mushin, Lagos State, Cerba Lancet’s CEO cited an 8–9% prevalence and about 325,000 new HBV infections in 2022. The community screening in Mushin Local Government attracted 247 participants and offered on-site testing, referrals and support. Officials emphasized early detection to prevent liver cancer and highlighted transmission routes including blood contact, unprotected sex and mother-to-child spread. While a vaccine exists, organizers stressed the need for stronger advocacy and culturally adapted education to close immunity gaps.

Karaole Semiu (Left); Cerba Lancet Nigeria MD, Dr Olayemi Dawodu and Mushin LG Medical Officer, Dr Kayode Odufuwa during a community health education and free screening programme Mushin Local Government Vice Chairman, Hon. Karaole Semiu (Left); Cerba Lancet Nigeria MD, Dr Olayemi Dawodu and Mushin LG Medical Officer, Dr Kayode Odufuwa during a community health education and free screening programme An estimated 18.2 million Nigerians are living with chronic hepatitis B, prompting health stakeholders to call for wider screening, vaccination and sustained public education to curb the burden of the viral infection. The call was made during a free medical screening and health education programme organised by Cerba Lancet in partnership with Mushin Local Council, Lagos State, as part of activities to commemorate the World Hepatitis Day, observed on July 28. The Managing Director and Chief Executive Officer of Cerba Lancet, Dr. Olayemi Dawodu, an anatomic and molecular pathologist, disclosed that the estimated prevalence of hepatitis B in Nigeria was about eight to nine per cent of the population. Dawodu, who spoke during the exercise, also said about 325,000 new hepatitis infections, predominantly hepatitis B virus (HBV), were estimated in 2022, citing the World Health Organisation (WHO) Regional Office for Africa. Against the backdrop of the disease burden, she said the community-based initiative was aimed at helping residents know their hepatitis status, access appropriate referrals and receive support where necessary. Held at the council’s multipurpose hall, the programme attracted 247 participants with combined activities, including hepatitis screening, health education, blood pressure and weight checks as well as random blood sugar testing. Dawodu said residents who tested positive for hepatitis B would be referred to secondary health facilities for proper management, while some participants would also receive support in the form of food and drugs. Explaining the need for early detection, Dawodu described hepatitis B as a viral infection that could lead to liver cancer if it was not detected and treated on time. She said the virus could be transmitted through contact with infected blood, unprotected sexual intercourse with an infected partner, exchange of needles and syringes, and the use of unclean equipment for piercing or tattooing. Dawodu also distinguished hepatitis B from hepatitis C, noting that although both viruses affect the liver and could result in chronic hepatitis, cirrhosis and liver cancer, their modes of transmission differ. “Hepatitis B is transmitted through blood, sexual contact and mother-to-child, while hepatitis C is transferred from blood to blood,” she said. On prevention, she stressed the importance of hepatitis B vaccination and urged people who had not received the vaccine to do so. However, Dawodu said the continued presence of hepatitis B despite the availability of a vaccine showed that vaccination needed to be complemented with stronger advocacy and health education. “We still need to do a lot of work by educating the communities in their local languages to enable them to understand the disease and how it spreads,” she said. According to her, better understanding of the routes of transmission would help Nigerians take steps to prevent infection. The Head of Business Development, Cerba Lancet, Temitope Ambrose, said Mushin was deliberately chosen for the exercise because it was where the company began its operations. He added that the area’s proximity to the Lagos University Teaching Hospital, which provides laboratory services, was also considered in selecting the location. Ambrose said the organisers initially planned to screen 200 people, but 247 residents eventually registered and participated in the exercise and described the turnout as encouraging, saying it showed the importance of taking health awareness and screening services closer to communities. Also, the Vice Chairman of Mushin Local Council, who supervises health, Semiu Ishola Karaole, commended Cerba Lancet for bringing the programme to residents. Karaole said the council chairman, Tunbosun Haruna Aruwe, regarded health as a priority and had assigned him responsibility for supervising the sector. The Medical Officer of the Local Council, Dr. Odufuwa Kayode, also emphasised the importance of early screening, describing hepatitis B as a common viral disease. Kayode said the disease could sometimes present with high fever and body pain, while its cumulative effects could make prevention more difficult. He described hepatitis B as a silent killer when not detected on time, saying it could cause inflammation of the liver and weaken other organs of the body. Kayode also said the Lagos State government had committed resources towards ensuring that residents were screened and treated on time. 美国成年人对甲型肝炎和乙型肝炎的免疫力低下,加上最近的政策变化可能预示着疾病的增加

Low immunity to hepatitis A and B in US adults plus recent policy changes may portend rise in disease
新闻简报发表在《JAMA Internal Medicine》上的一项研究显示,美国成年人对甲型肝炎和乙型肝炎的血清学免疫力较低,其中 39.5% 对 HAV 免疫,27.1% 对 HBV 免疫。该研究基于 2017 年至 2023 年的数据,强调了与年龄、种族、教育程度和出生地相关的免疫力差异。2025 年 12 月,CDC 和 ACIP 更新了 HBV 疫苗接种 指南,将低风险婴儿的通用出生剂量 疫苗接种 转变为基于个人的决策。研究人员指出,这种变化可能会导致肝炎发病率增加,他们强调需要积极采取 疫苗接种 措施来预防肝硬化、肝衰竭和肝细胞癌等严重后果。该调查结果可作为评估新政策影响的基线,由于 疫苗接种 覆盖范围的减少和预防性医疗保健服务的潜在中断,新政策引起了人们对公共卫生风险的担忧。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究数据涵盖时间段为2017年1月至2023年8月,CDC政策变更发生于2025年12月。文章发表于2026-08-18。
地点与对象
该研究主要针对美国成年人,特别提到了高危人群。参与者的平均年龄为 48.7 岁,其中 51.8% 为女性。
事件
发表在《JAMA Internal Medicine》上的一项研究发现,美国成年人(包括感染高危人群)对甲型肝炎病毒 (HAV) 和 乙型肝炎病毒 (HBV) 的血清学免疫力较低。CDC 和 ACIP 于 2025 年 12 月更新了指南,建议对未感染 HBV 的母亲所生的婴儿进行基于个体的 HBV 疫苗接种 决策,取代通用出生剂量 疫苗接种。
规模、影响与风险
39.5%的成年人对HAV有血清学免疫,27.1%对HBV有血清学免疫,25.2%表现出疫苗衍生的免疫。在所有成年人中,66.9%被归类为高风险。政策变化可能导致肝炎发病率上升,慢性乙型肝炎感染与肝硬化、肝功能衰竭和肝细胞癌相关。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究为评估疾病预防控制中心的政策变化建立了基线。研究人员建议维持基于证据的 疫苗接种 策略,以防止严重的长期后遗症。政策变化的影响尚不清楚,但建议加大 疫苗接种 的力度。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Low immunity to hepatitis A and B in US adults plus recent policy changes may portend rise in disease

News Brief
A study published in JAMA Internal Medicine reveals low serologic immunity to hepatitis A and B among US adults, with 39.5% immune to HAV and 27.1% immune to HBV. The research, based on data from 2017 to 2023, highlights disparities in immunity linked to age, race, education, and birthplace. In December 2025, the CDC and ACIP updated HBV vaccination guidelines, shifting from universal birth-dose vaccination to individual-based decision-making for low-risk infants. This change may lead to increased hepatitis rates, as noted by the researchers, who emphasize the need for active vaccination efforts to prevent severe outcomes like cirrhosis, liver failure, and hepatocellular carcinoma. The findings serve as a baseline for assessing the impact of the new policy, which has raised concerns about public health risks due to reduced vaccination coverage and potential disruptions in preventive healthcare access.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content Main navigation News Antimicrobial Resistance COVID-19 Chronic Wasting Disease Influenza Public Health Alerts All Topics A-Z Coronavirus Vaccines Roadmap Influenza Vaccines Roadmap Roadmaps for Priority Pathogens Vaccine Integrity Project CIDRAP Leadership Forum . . Topics & Projects Antimicrobial Stewardship Chronic Wasting Disease Osterholm Update Podcasts Newsletters About CIDRAP CIDRAP in the News Our Staff Our Director Osterholm in the Press Shop Merchandise Contact Us About Search CLOSE Search Support Menu Low immunity to hepatitis A and B in US adults plus recent policy changes may portend rise in disease Mary Van Beusekom, MS Hepatitis Childhood Vaccines Rasi Bhadramani / iStock Share Copied to clipboard A study published yesterday in JAMA Internal Medicine reveals low serologic immunity to hepatitis A virus (HAV) and hepatitis B virus (HBV) among US adults, including those at high risk for infection. Using data from the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey from January 2017 to August 2023, researchers from the University of Minnesota and the Minneapolis Veterans Affairs Health Care System evaluated HAV and HBV serologic test results from 13,514 adults aged 20 and older for the presence of antibodies from previous infection or vaccination. “Universal birth-dose HBV vaccination, adopted in the US in 1991, was associated with a 95% reduction in infant HBV infections, preventing approximately 90 000 deaths,” they wrote. “However, in December 2025, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) and the Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices (ACIP) updated guidelines to recommend individual-based decision-making for infants of mothers without HBV infection, replacing universal birth-dose vaccination,” they added. Findings establish baseline for evaluating policy change The average participant age was 48.7 years, and 51.8% were women. In total, 39.5% of adults were serologically immune to HAV, 27.1% had serologic immunity to HBV, and 25.2% showed vaccine-derived immunity. Safe and effective vaccines prevent HAV and HBV infections, and serologic immunity protects high-risk populations from severe outcomes. HAV immunity was tied to younger age (adjusted odds ratio [aOR], 0.51); lower educational attainment (aOR, 0.32); and Black (aOR, 1.67), Mexican-American (aOR, 4.22), other Hispanic (aOR, 2.01), Asian (aOR, 3.03), and multiracial or other race and ethnicity (aOR, 1.36); as well as birth outside the United States (aOR, 4.54) and awareness of liver disease (OR, 1.65). Obesity was linked to lower odds of being immune to HAV (aOR, 0.83). Vaccine-derived HBV immunity was associated with younger age (aOR, 0.18), female sex (aOR, 1.36), Asian (aOR, 1.47) and Black (aOR, 1.15) race, and higher educational attainment (aOR, 3.29). Among all adults, 66.9% were categorized as high risk. In these subgroups, HAV immunity ranged from 26.7% for metabolic dysfunction–linked alcohol-related liver disease to 63.7% for chronic HBV infection, while vaccine-derived HBV immunity ranged from 14.0% for chronic kidney disease to 38.6% for pregnancy. The low rates of immunity, which previous studies have also found, may be explained by the aging of naturally immune cohorts who had HAV in childhood, when infection was more common; suboptimal HAV vaccination coverage among younger adults; and COVID-19 pandemic disruptions to preventive healthcare access, the authors said. The findings establish a baseline for evaluating the CDC policy change from universal birth-dose HBV vaccination to shared clinical decision-making for low-risk infants, they added. “Safe and effective vaccines prevent HAV and HBV infections, and serologic immunity protects high-risk populations from severe outcomes,” the researchers wrote. “In light of growing public concern regarding vaccine safety and benefit and recent changes in ACIP HBV vaccination policy, maintaining evidence-based vaccination strategies remains essential.” Increased vaccination efforts needed In an editorial published in the same journal, Melinda Wang, MD, MHS, of the University of California San Francisco, and colleagues, note that infants are particularly vulnerable to hepatitis, with roughly 90% of those exposed to HBV developing chronic disease, compared with 30% of children aged 1 to 5 years and 5% of adults. Although chronic hepatitis B can be treated, it is both incurable and may be associated with cirrhosis, liver failure, and hepatocellular carcinoma. “Although chronic hepatitis B can be treated, it is both incurable and may be associated with cirrhosis, liver failure, and hepatocellular carcinoma,” they wrote. While the effects of the 2025 hepatitis vaccine policy change aren’t yet clear, it is likely to lead to increased rates of hepatitis, they added. The study data spans from January 2017 to August 2023, with the CDC policy change occurring in December 2025. The article was published on 2026-08-18.
Location and population
The study focused on US adults, with a specific mention of high-risk populations. The average participant age was 48.7 years, and 51.8% were women.
Event
A study published in JAMA Internal Medicine found low serologic immunity to hepatitis A virus (HAV) and hepatitis B virus (HBV) among US adults, including those at high risk for infection. The CDC and ACIP updated guidelines in December 2025 to recommend individual-based decision-making for HBV vaccination in infants of mothers without HBV infection, replacing universal birth-dose vaccination.
Scale, impact and risk
39.5% of adults were serologically immune to HAV, 27.1% had serologic immunity to HBV, and 25.2% showed vaccine-derived immunity. Among all adults, 66.9% were categorized as high risk. The policy change may lead to increased rates of hepatitis, with chronic HBV infection associated with cirrhosis, liver failure, and hepatocellular carcinoma.
Response, status and uncertainty
The study establishes a baseline for evaluating the CDC policy change. The researchers suggest maintaining evidence-based vaccination strategies to prevent serious long-term sequelae. The implications of the policy change are not yet clear, but increased vaccination efforts are recommended.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Detecting Hepatitis B Among Pregnant Women: A Growing Concern at Ho Teaching Hospital - Modern Ghana

News Brief
A research project at Ho Teaching Hospital and Ho Municipal Hospital in Ghana is investigating Hepatitis B virus infection among pregnant women attending antenatal care. The study, part of an undergraduate research project in Biochemistry and Molecular Biology, aims to detect the Hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg) and examine factors associated with infection. Preliminary findings from the first 90 participants tested show 13 positive results and 77 negative results, but these are only a fraction of the planned 300 participants. The research highlights the importance of early detection to prevent mother-to-child transmission and improve maternal and newborn health. However, recruitment challenges have slowed data collection, and the study is ongoing with further testing planned for positive cases. The findings should be interpreted cautiously as more data are needed to draw meaningful conclusions about the prevalence of Hepatitis B among pregnant women in the region.
Original source text (English)
☰ ✕ HOME NEWS SPORTS SHOWBIZ AFRICA WORLD BUSINESS OPINIONS POLITICS LIFESTYLE HEALTH Close the sidebar NEWS HEALTH BUSINESS POLITICS OPINIONS SHOWBIZ SPORTS AFRICA WORLD Detecting Hepatitis B Among Pregnant Women: A Growing Concern at Ho Teaching Hospital An ongoing study at the Ho Teaching Hospital aims to identify cases of Hepatitis B among pregnant women, promoting early diagnosis and ensuring timely, appropriate care for affected mothers and their babies. By Ankumah Fred 18 Aug 2026 Hepatitis B remains a significant public health concern, particularly among pregnant women. An infected mother can transmit the virus to her baby during pregnancy or childbirth, making early detection an essential part of protecting both maternal and newborn health. At the Ho Teaching Hospital, an ongoing research project is investigating Hepatitis B virus infection among pregnant women attending antenatal care. The study seeks to determine the presence of the Hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg) among pregnant women and examine factors that may be associated with infection. The study, titled “Detection of Hepatitis B Virus Among Pregnant Women in the Ho Teaching Hospital,” is being conducted as part of an undergraduate research project in Biochemistry and Molecular Biology. The project is expected to involve 300 pregnant women recruited during antenatal visits. Participants are provided with information about the study before samples are collected for testing. The research is expected to generate useful data on the occurrence of Hepatitis B infection among pregnant women receiving care at the hospital. Figure 1: Study process for detection of Hepatitis B among pregnant women Preliminary findings from the first 90 participants tested have identified 13 positive Hepatitis B results, while 77 participants tested negative. These early findings represent only a portion of the intended study population and should not be interpreted as the overall prevalence of Hepatitis B among pregnant women at the hospital. Nevertheless, the preliminary results highlight the importance of continued screening and awareness among pregnant women. Detecting the infection during pregnancy creates an opportunity for timely medical advice, appropriate clinical follow‐up, and measures aimed at reducing the risk of mother‐to‐child transmission. The research has, however, faced challenges in recruiting participants. The number of pregnant women attending antenatal services at the study site has been lower than expected, slowing the pace of data collection and making it more difficult to reach the intended sample size within the expected timeframe. To improve recruitment, the research team extended sample collection to the Ho Municipal Hospital. This move is intended to increase access to pregnant women attending antenatal care and support the study in reaching its target number of participants. Despite this effort, balancing fieldwork with academic responsibilities has presented another challenge. Many pregnant women report for antenatal care at times that overlap with the researcher’s lecture schedule. Managing these competing responsibilities has therefore become an important part of the research process. The study uses rapid diagnostic testing as an initial method of detecting Hepatitis B infection. Participants who test positive may undergo further testing to confirm their status. This approach allows the research team to identify individuals who may require additional clinical attention. Beyond detecting infection, the study also collects information that may help researchers understand factors associated with Hepatitis B among pregnant women. This includes sociodemographic characteristics, obstetric history, and selected behavioural practices. For pregnant women, Hepatitis B screening should not be viewed simply as another medical test. Knowing one’s Hepatitis B status provides an opportunity for timely medical advice and appropriate interventions. It also helps prevent an infected mother from unknowingly transmitting the virus to her newborn. The preliminary findings from the Ho Teaching Hospital should therefore be interpreted with caution. With only 90 participants tested out of the planned 300, more data are required before meaningful conclusions can be drawn about the extent of Hepatitis B infection within the study population. As sample collection continues, the research is expected to provide a clearer picture of Hepatitis B infection among pregnant women attending antenatal care. The findings could contribute to greater awareness of the importance of Hepatitis B screening during pregnancy and reinforce the need for early detection and appropriate follow‐up. Ultimately, the study serves as a reminder that behind every test result is a mother and a child whose health can be affected by an infection that requires timely attention. Continued screening, public education, and appropriate healthcare interventions remain essential in reducing the impact of Hepatitis B on mothers, newborns, and families. Healthy mothers. Healthy babies. A future free from Hepatitis B. Ankumah Fred Biochemistry and Molecular Biology University of Health and Allied Sciences (UHAS), Ghana Comments19 Ad

慢性乙型肝炎 市场报告提供了对市场 进化 和增长前景的见解 - openPR.com

Chronic Hepatitis B Market Report Provides Insights Into Market Evolution And Growth Prospects - openPR.com
新闻简报This report provides an overview of the chronic hepatitis B market, highlighting its steady growth trajectory from $3.93 billion in 2025 to an expected $4.74 billion by 2030, with a compound annual growth rate (CAGR) of 3.7%. The growth is attributed to advancements in therapy, increased global health initiatives, and the development of next-generation HBV treatments, including therapeutic vaccines and innovative antiviral drugs like bepirovirsen. The report also notes the acquisition of a hepatitis B therapy by GlaxoSmithKline (GSK) in November 2023, which includes an antisense oligonucleotide drug, ARO-HBV, designed to target the hepatitis B virus. Key trends include the adoption of long-term antiviral therapies, functional cure strategies, and improved patient adherence programs. The report segments the market by drug class, gender, and distribution channel, offering detailed insights into market dynamics and future growth prospects. It does not provide any specific public health response or investigation details, focusing instead on market analysis and industry trends.

该报告发布于2026-08-17。市场增长预测涵盖2025年至2030年期间,其中特别提到2026年增长率为4.2%复合年增长率(CAGR)。
地点与对象
该报告的范围是全球性的,重点关注不同地区和人群的 慢性乙型肝炎 市场,包括性别和不同的分销渠道。
事件
该报告讨论了在治疗和全球健康倡议的进步推动下 慢性乙型肝炎 市场的增长。它重点介绍了新的 治疗 选项、治疗性 疫苗 的开发以及制药公司对乙型肝炎疗法的收购。
规模、影响与风险
The chronic hepatitis B market is forecasted to reach $4.74 billion by 2030, growing at a CAGR of 3.7%. It is expected to grow from $3.93 billion in 2025 to $4.1 billion in 2026 at a CAGR of 4.2%. The report notes the increasing demand for treatments with finite durations and the development of therapies targeting viral replication and immune control.
应对、状态与不确定性
该报告是一份市场分析,没有提及任何具体的公共卫生应对措施或调查。它提供了对市场趋势和增长的一般见解,没有任何不确定性或未解决信息的迹象。

Chronic Hepatitis B Market Report Provides Insights Into Market Evolution And Growth Prospects The chronic hepatitis B market is on a steady growth path, driven by advancements in therapy and increasing global health initiatives. As new treatment options and innovative approaches emerge, this sector is expected to expand steadily over the coming years. Let's explore the anticipated market valuation, key players, evolving trends, and detailed segment breakdowns shaping the industry's future. Projected Market Valuation for Chronic Hepatitis B by 2030 The chronic hepatitis B market is forecasted to reach a valuation of $4.74 billion by 2030, growing at a compound annual growth rate (CAGR) of 3.7%. This upward trajectory is primarily fueled by rising investments in next-generation HBV therapies and the growing demand for treatments with finite durations. Additionally, the development of therapeutic vaccines is gaining momentum, alongside increased efforts to eliminate viral reservoirs. Global initiatives aimed at hepatitis eradication further support this growth outlook. Key trends expected to influence the market include widespread adoption of long-term antiviral therapies, a focus on developing functional cure strategies, enhanced early detection and monitoring of the disease, expansion of immune-modulating treatments, and the implementation of programs to improve patient adherence. The chronic hepatitis b market size has grown steadily in recent years. It will grow from $3.93 billion in 2025 to $4.1 billion in 2026 at a compound annual growth rate (CAGR) of 4.2%. The growth in the historic period can be attributed to improved hepatitis B screening programs, expansion of antiviral drug availability, rising awareness of liver disease complications, increased use of nucleos(t)ide analogs, establishment of long-term disease monitoring protocols. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

悲惨的损失如何让 Missiani Ochwoto 博士成为肯尼亚领先的乙型肝炎十字军 - Streamlinefeed.co.ke

How a Tragic Loss Turned Dr. Missiani Ochwoto into Kenya's Leading Hepatitis B Crusader - streamlinefeed.co.ke
新闻简报Dr. Missiani Ochwoto, a senior virologist at Kenya’s Medical Research Institute (KEMRI), has transformed personal tragedy into a national crusade against hepatitis B. After witnessing a neighbor’s family succumb to the virus in northwestern Kenya, he dedicated his career to combating HBV, which infects roughly one million Kenyans and causes up to 1.2 million global deaths annually. Ochwoto champions the birth-dose vaccine within 24 hours of birth and antiviral treatment for HBV-positive mothers, with pilot programs in Baringo County showing zero transmission to newborns. Yet, rural diagnostic gaps and pervasive stigma—comparable to HIV—continue to obstruct progress. Despite WHO’s 2030 elimination target, Ochwoto warns that underfunding and weak infrastructure threaten Kenya’s ability to break transmission chains and save lives.
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 8 月 17 日发布;事件被描述为在肯尼亚西北部的年轻人的历史背景下持续进行的。
地点与对象
肯尼亚,区域重点是图尔卡纳县和巴林戈县;受影响人群包括高患病率地区的母亲和婴儿以及不了解乙肝病毒状况的一般人群。
事件
KEMRI 的高级病毒学家 Missiani Ochwoto 在邻居家人因乙型肝炎去世后,领导肯尼亚抗击乙型肝炎,将其作为个人的十字军东征;这篇文章介绍了他的倡导、研究和预防工作。
规模、影响与风险
National HBV prevalence ~3.5% (~1 million infected); regional prevalence up to 7.6% in Turkana and Baringo; globally 254 million chronic HBV cases and up to 1.2 million deaths annually; HBV is second leading cause of cancer deaths in Kenya; 90% risk of chronic infection if infants contract HBV at birth; underfunding relative to HIV, TB, and malaria.
应对、状态与不确定性
KEMRI 在巴林戈县领导的试点项目显示,通过出生剂量 疫苗接种 和 抗病毒药物 治疗 成功预防了母婴 传播;扩大即时诊断和减少耻辱是当前的优先事项;政策制定者的资金分配和基础设施升级仍未得到充分调查和解决。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

How a Tragic Loss Turned Dr. Missiani Ochwoto into Kenya's Leading Hepatitis B Crusader - streamlinefeed.co.ke

News Brief
Dr. Missiani Ochwoto, a senior virologist at Kenya’s Medical Research Institute (KEMRI), has transformed personal tragedy into a national crusade against hepatitis B. After witnessing a neighbor’s family succumb to the virus in northwestern Kenya, he dedicated his career to combating HBV, which infects roughly one million Kenyans and causes up to 1.2 million global deaths annually. Ochwoto champions the birth-dose vaccine within 24 hours of birth and antiviral treatment for HBV-positive mothers, with pilot programs in Baringo County showing zero transmission to newborns. Yet, rural diagnostic gaps and pervasive stigma—comparable to HIV—continue to obstruct progress. Despite WHO’s 2030 elimination target, Ochwoto warns that underfunding and weak infrastructure threaten Kenya’s ability to break transmission chains and save lives.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content Global editionThursday, August 20, 2026 Streamline Search⌘KWriteSign inJoin Streamline StreamlineGlobal edition LatestWorldBusinessPoliticsTechnologyScienceCultureSportDirectoryHouse HuntingPeopleHistoryMore LatestWorldBusinessPoliticsTechnologyScienceCultureSport Back to News/Health Health How a Tragic Loss Turned Dr. Missiani Ochwoto into Kenya's Leading Hepatitis B Crusader Dr. Missiani Ochwoto, a senior KEMRI virologist, is leading Kenya's fight against the Hepatitis B epidemic, driven by a childhood tragedy that wiped out a neighbor's family. Streamline Feed OfficialVerified Aug 17, 2026Updated Aug 17, 20264 min read Follow on Google News Try askingGive me the 30-second brief Build your reading streak0/3 today·Counts at 25% For Dr. Missiani Ochwoto, the fight against Hepatitis B is not merely a scientific endeavor; it is a deeply personal crusade forged in tragedy. Now a senior virologist and medical research scientist at the Kenya Medical Research Institute (KEMRI), Dr. Ochwoto's career trajectory was permanently altered during his youth in northwestern Kenya, when a devastating disease wiped out the family of his closest friend. That disease was the Hepatitis B virus (HBV), a silent epidemic that continues to claim thousands of lives across the continent. The catalyst for his lifelong mission occurred when his neighbor—a young mother of two—returned home after contracting the virus while working in northern Kenya. Misunderstood and heavily stigmatized, she was divorced by her husband following her diagnosis. She suffered from a distended abdomen, a classic symptom of severe liver disease, and ultimately succumbed to the infection. Two years later, the virus claimed the lives of both her young sons. The realization that an entire generation of a single family had been eradicated by a preventable virus pushed Dr. Ochwoto to dedicate his life to understanding, diagnosing, and fighting viral hepatitis. The Silent Epidemic in Kenya Today, Dr. Ochwoto stands at the forefront of Kenya's battle against a disease that remains critically underfunded compared to HIV, tuberculosis, and malaria. Hepatitis B is a highly infectious viral infection that attacks the liver, often leading to chronic fibrosis, cirrhosis, and liver cancer—the second leading cause of cancer deaths in Kenya. National Prevalence: According to data from the World Health Organization (WHO) and the Kenya Population-based HIV Impact Assessment, the national prevalence of Hepatitis B in Kenya stands at approximately 3.5 percent, translating to roughly one million infected individuals. Regional Disparities: Certain counties carry a disproportionately high burden. Regions such as Turkana and Baringo record prevalence rates as high as 7.6 percent, driven by historical lack of access to early infant vaccination and poor diagnostic infrastructure. Global Context: Globally, the WHO reports that 254 million people suffer from chronic HBV, resulting in up to 1.2 million deaths annually. Despite these staggering numbers, Dr. Ochwoto warns that many Kenyans remain entirely unaware of their status. The infection often presents with no symptoms in its early stages, or mimics common, less severe illnesses. By the time physical symptoms such as jaundice (yellowing of the eyes), dark skin blotches, or a distended abdomen appear, the liver is usually already severely damaged. Breaking the Chain of Transmission A significant portion of Dr. Ochwoto's current work at KEMRI focuses on the prevention of Mother-To-Child Transmission (MTCT). In regions with endemic infection rates, babies born to HBV-positive mothers are at extreme risk. If an infant contracts the virus at birth, there is a 90 percent chance the infection will become chronic, setting the stage for liver failure in adulthood. To combat this, Dr. Ochwoto and his colleagues have championed the strict implementation of the Hepatitis B birth-dose vaccine. When administered within the first 24 hours of life, followed by the standard pentavalent vaccine schedule at 6, 10, and 14 weeks, the birth dose is highly effective at halting vertical transmission. Recent pilot programs in Baringo County have demonstrated remarkable success; mothers who tested positive during antenatal clinic visits were provided with viral-suppressing treatments, and their newborns received immediate postnatal vaccinations. Follow-up testing consistently showed these infants remaining virus-free. The Road Ahead: Diagnostics and Stigma Beyond vaccination, Dr. Ochwoto is fighting to expand Point-Of-Care (POC) diagnostic testing across rural Kenya. Currently, many rural clinics lack the advanced serological equipment needed to detect the Hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg) accurately. Misdiagnosis is rampant; patients presenting with fatigue and erratic symptoms are frequently tested for HIV and sent home when the results return negative, completely missing the underlying liver infection. Furthermore, the social stigma surrounding the disease remains a formidable barrier. Because Hepatitis B is transmitted through contact with infected blood and bodily fluids—including unprotected sex—it carries a stigma similar to HIV. Many diagnosed patients hide their status, refusing life-saving antiviral medications for fear of social ostracization or job loss. As Kenya and the global medical community push toward the WHO's goal of eliminating viral hepatitis as a major public health threat by 2030, scientists like Dr. Missiani Ochwoto remain the critical vanguard. Driven by the memory of a neighbor who died misunderstood and alone, he continues to demand that policymakers allocate the necessary funding, upgrade testing infrastructure, and ensure that no mother ever has to wonder if she will pass a lethal, preventable virus to her unborn child. 新闻简报2026 年 8 月 15 日,加纳国家病毒性肝炎控制项目项目经理 John Gerald Adiiboka 博士警告说,理发、纹身和包皮环切手术中重复使用、消毒不充分的设备可能会带来乙型肝炎 传播 的风险。他强调,如果工具没有得到适当清洁,这些程序可能会使个人接触受感染的血液。这一警告是在加纳乙型肝炎的巨大隐性负担之际发出的,估计有 300 万人受到感染,但只有约 1% 的人被诊断出来。据估计,该病毒的 患病率 占人口的 8% 到 9%。Adiiboka博士强调,母婴传播是主要途径,约占该国感染的95%。他建议个人确保设备经过消毒或使用个人工具来降低风险。低 诊断 率是控制病毒的主要障碍,因为许多人仍然不知道自己的状况。该警告基于已确认的公共卫生信息,明确说明了风险,但未对上述 传播 路线之外的风险进行量化。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Hepatitis B: How your barber’s blade could put you at risk - GhanaWeb

News Brief
On August 15, 2026, Dr John Gerald Adiiboka, Programme Manager of the National Viral Hepatitis Control Programme in Ghana, warned about the risk of Hepatitis B transmission through reused, inadequately sterilised equipment in barbering, tattooing, and circumcision practices. He highlighted that these procedures can expose individuals to infected blood if the tools are not properly cleaned. The warning comes amid a significant hidden burden of Hepatitis B in Ghana, with an estimated three million people infected, though only about one percent have been diagnosed. The prevalence of the virus is estimated at between eight and nine percent of the population. Dr Adiiboka stressed that mother-to-child transmission is the dominant route, accounting for about 95% of infections in the country. He advised individuals to ensure equipment is sterilised or use personal tools to reduce risk. The low diagnosis rate is a major obstacle to controlling the virus, as many people remain unaware of their status. The warning is based on confirmed public health information, and the risk is explicitly stated but not quantified beyond the mentioned transmission routes.

Please wait, loading captcha By continuing, you agree to the Terms & Privacy Policy Self Service Advert Sitemap Partners About Us Sign up Login Home News Sports Business Entertainment Africa GhanaWeb TV Opinions Memories Cartoons Say It Loud Real Estate Home - NewsElection 2024TWI News | TVPollsPress ReleaseNews BlogsNews ArchiveCrime & PunishmentPolitics More RegionalEditorialHealthGhanaians AbroadTabloidAfricaReligionCoronavirusNews Videos | TVPhoto ArchivesNews HeadlinesCartoonsYear In ReviewEyes On The Ground | TVThe Untold | TVPeople & Places | TVThe Lowdown | TVSay It Loud | TV News You are here: Home → News → 2026 08 15 → Article 2047829 General News of Saturday, 15 August 2026 Disclaimer Source: theheraldghana.com 2026-08-15 Hepatitis B: How your barber’s blade could put you at risk « Prev Next » Comments (4) Listen to Article Share: Diagram detailing effects of Hepatitis B on liver A seemingly harmless visit to the barber, beauty salon or tattoo centre could expose people to Hepatitis B if equipment contaminated with infected blood is reused without proper sterilisation, the Programme Manager of the National Viral Hepatitis Control Programme, Dr John Gerald Adiiboka, has warned. Dr Adiiboka said the virus, which is transmitted primarily through contact with infected blood and certain body fluids, could be spread through practices involving shared or inadequately sterilised blades, needles and other instruments. He specifically mentioned barbering, tattooing and circumcision among activities where people could unknowingly be exposed to infected blood. “You can get one person who would use one blade to circumcise or give tribal marks or probably carry out a procedure on different people,” Dr Adiiboka said. He explained that tattooing could also pose a risk, particularly where the procedure causes bleeding and the equipment is merely cleaned rather than properly sterilised before being used on another person. “There are some tattoos where you bleed. If you are bleeding from the accident, you just clean the machine. You don’t sterilise it. And they are going to use it for another person,” he cautioned. The same concern, he said, applied to barbering shops, where customers could suffer minor cuts during shaving and grooming. According to Dr. Adiiboka, if equipment used on a bleeding customer is not adequately sterilised before being used on another person, infected blood could provide a route for transmission. He, therefore, advised people to take precautions when patronising barbering services, including ensuring that equipment is properly sterilised or using personal equipment where possible. “I always advise people you have to modify your own barbering machine or you go to a place where the barber is [properly sterilising the equipment],” he said, disclosing that he personally owns his barbering equipment. Dr Adiiboka’s warning comes amid what he described as a significant hidden Hepatitis B burden in Ghana, with an estimated three million people believed to be infected. He said the country had, however, diagnosed only about one percent of the estimated infected population, leaving a vast majority unaware of their status. Explaining how the estimated number was derived, he said prevalence studies were used alongside Ghana’s population data to arrive at an estimated national burden. He said Ghana’s Hepatitis B prevalence was estimated at between eight and nine percent, meaning that in a population of 100 people, roughly eight or nine could be infected in a population reflecting that prevalence. Dr Adiiboka said the low diagnosis rate posed a major obstacle to Ghana’s efforts to control and eventually eliminate viral hepatitis. “If you say three million Ghanaians are infected, and then you want to equate it to percentage wise, the prevalence in Ghana is between eight to nine percent,” he explained. He identified lack of awareness and stigma as some of the major barriers preventing people from seeking testing. “There are a lot of people who are walking about, they don’t know. They get it. So they are not aware,” he said. According to him, some people also associate Hepatitis B with sexually transmitted infections and consequently avoid testing because of the stigma attached to such diseases. He, however, clarified that sexual transmission was not the main mode of transmission of Hepatitis B in Ghana. Ghanaregional profiles Rather, he said, mother-to-child transmission was the dominant route, accounting for about 95% of infections in Ghana. Dr. Adiiboka explained that babies exposed to the virus during childbirth were particularly vulnerable because their immature immune systems were less capable of clearing the infection. “When a baby is born and is exposed to the virus during childbirth, the child has 95% chance of getting the virus,” he said. Adults, he explained, were more likely to clear the virus following exposure because of their stronger immune systems. However, blood-to-blood exposure, sexual contact and the sharing of contaminated needles or instruments remain possible routes of infection. He also sought to dispel misconceptions about ordinary social interaction with people living with Hepatitis B. According to him, hugging, sharing food or utensils and ordinary kissing do not constitute common routes of transmission. He said the concern with kissing would arise where there was blood-to-blood contact, while saliva itself contained only a minimal amount of the virus. Beyond transmission, Dr. Adiiboka raised concerns about the financial burden confronting people who test positive for Hepatitis B. He said while screening tests could be covered under the National Health Insurance Scheme (NHIS), several advanced investigations required after a positive diagnosis were not covered. Patients may require liver and kidney function tests, viral-load testing, imaging and other investigations before treatment can begin. He estimated that the initial process from testing positive through the investigations required to start medication could cost between GH¢800 and GH¢1,500, depending on the tests required. The financial burden could continue because some people with chronic Hepatitis B require long-term antiviral medication. Dr Adiiboka said some medicines could cost between GH¢150 and GH¢300 a month, with certain brands reaching about GH¢400. He said efforts were underway with non-governmental organisations and other partners to make the medication more affordable, with an option costing about GH¢70 per month currently being worked on. Unlike Hepatitis C, which can be cured, chronic Hepatitis B is generally managed with long-term medication for patients who require treatment. “If you put [the patient] on the medication, you don’t stop. As long as you are on the medication, you are fine. But once you stop the medication, it means that the virus is likely to come back,” he said. Dr. Adiiboka warned that failure to diagnose and properly manage chronic Hepatitis B could result in serious complications, including liver cirrhosis, liver failure and liver cancer. He said liver cancer was a major cause of cancer deaths in Ghana and ranked among the leading cancers affecting men. More worrying, he said, was that serious “The average is about 42 years,” he said, referring to the age at which complications such as liver failure, liver cancer and cirrhosis could develop. He warned that when a breadwinner or economically productive person develops severe complications, the consequences extend beyond the individual to families, communities and the national economy. “The negative burden is quite heavy,” he stressed. Dr. Adiiboka said Ghana would need to dramatically expand testing and treatment to move closer to the World Health Organisation’s 2030 viral hepatitis elimination target. He said the global target requires countries to diagnose at least 90% of people living with chronic viral hepatitis and ensure that at least 80% of those diagnosed receive appropriate treatment. Ghana, he acknowledged, remains far from the target, with diagnosis estimated at only about one percent. He said expanding population-wide screening, improving access to affordable medicines and strengthening prevention would therefore be critical. Dr Adiiboka also highlighted Ghana’s efforts to prevent mother-to-child transmission through vaccination, including the introduction of Hepatitis B vaccination into the routine childhood immunisation programme and efforts to provide a birth dose within 24 hours of delivery. For the public, however, his warning extends beyond hospitals and vaccination centres: instruments that cut, pierce or come into contact with blood must be properly sterilised. A barber’s blade may appear to be just another tool of grooming, but if contaminated with infected blood, Dr Adiiboka’s warning suggests, it could become a vehicle for a virus that a person may carry for life. 18.2m Nigerians living with chronic Hepatitis B - The Nation Newspaper

18.2m Nigerians living with chronic Hepatitis B - The Nation Newspaper
新闻简报The article reports that an estimated 18.2 million Nigerians are living with chronic hepatitis B, many of whom are unaware of their status. This was highlighted by Dr Olayemi Dawodu, Managing Director of Cerba Lancet Nigeria, during a free hepatitis B screening and health education program in Mushin Local Government Area on August 18, 2026, marking World Hepatitis Day. The event, organized in partnership with local authorities, attracted 247 participants, surpassing the expected 200. Dawodu emphasized the importance of expanding community-level screening, vaccination, and public education efforts, particularly through health education in local languages to enhance understanding of hepatitis B transmission and prevention. The findings underscore the ongoing challenges in addressing the public health burden of hepatitis B in Nigeria.

Thursday, August 20, 2026 Advertise With Us E-paper Follow Us on Google × Home News Entertainment Business Politics Sports Inside Africa Editorial Columnists City Beats Foreign Contact Us News NigeriaNews Alerts Follow Us on Google August 18, 2026 by Chinyere Okoroafor 18.2m Nigerians living with chronic Hepatitis B Discover more Africans & Diaspora Health health • ‘Screening gaps persist’ An estimated 18.2 million Nigerians are living with chronic hepatitis B, with many unaware of their status, the Managing Director of Cerba Lancet Nigeria, Dr Olayemi Dawodu, has said. Dawodu, an anatomic and molecular pathologist, disclosed this during a free hepatitis B screening and health education programme organised by Cerba Lancet Nigeria in partnership with Mushin Local Government Area to mark World Hepatitis Day. NigerianHealth News She said Nigeria’s hepatitis B prevalence was estimated at between eight and nine per cent, while about 325,000 new hepatitis infections, predominantly hepatitis B, were estimated in 2022, citing the World Health Organisation Regional Office for Africa. The screening attracted 247 residents, exceeding the organisers’ initial target of 200 participants. Dawodu said the turnout underscored the need to expand community-level screening, vaccination and public education. “Know your status. Get your hepatitis B status, do the test and know whether you are positive or negative. If you are positive, there is management,” she said. She said residents who tested positive would be referred to secondary healthcare facilities for further management. Discover more Newspaper History Newspapers Dawodu urged stronger health education, particularly in local languages, to improve understanding of hepatitis B transmission and prevention. Health She said the infection could be transmitted through infected blood and body fluids, sexual contact, contaminated needles and sharp objects, as well as from mother to child. Vice Chairman of Mushin Local Government Area, Semiu Ishola Karaole, commended the initiative, saying the council remained committed to improving healthcare delivery. He said the council has seven Primary Health Care Centres and four functional ambulances. Location and population
Nigeria, with a focus on Mushin Local Government Area.
Event
An estimated 18.2 million Nigerians are living with chronic hepatitis B, many unaware of their status, as reported by Dr Olayemi Dawodu, Managing Director of Cerba Lancet Nigeria.

The event highlights the need for expanded community-level screening, vaccination, and public education.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Doctors encourage Albertans to get tested for hepatitis B - ctvnews.ca

News Brief
Alberta health professionals are urging residents to get tested for hepatitis B after a recent survey showed that almost nine out of ten Canadians have little knowledge of the virus. The call to action, reported on August 19, 2026, emphasizes the importance of screening to identify undiagnosed infections, even though no specific case numbers or outbreaks are mentioned. By raising awareness, officials aim to reduce the hidden burden of hepatitis B in the province. The guidance reflects a preventive public‐health approach rather than a response to an active outbreak, and no further investigations or treatment details are provided in the report.

所提供的证据中未报告。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Doctors calling for wider Hepatitis B testing | CTV News Red Deer: Aug. 19, 2026 - ctvnews.ca

News Brief
On August 19, 2026, some Alberta doctors urged for wider Hepatitis B testing, highlighting that the virus may be present without individuals being aware. The article, published by CTV News, emphasizes the importance of identifying and addressing Hepatitis B infections, which can lead to serious health complications. While the piece does not provide specific case counts or death tolls, it underscores the need for increased public health awareness and proactive testing strategies. The call for testing is part of an ongoing discussion about the virus's prevalence and the potential risks associated with undiagnosed infections. The article does not mention any official response or confirmed outbreak, but it suggests that the issue is under investigation and that further action may be required to address the public health concern.
